					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Spanish priest and mystic (1542–1591)</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the Spanish mystic and saint. For the national personification of the Philippines, see <a href="/wiki/Juan_dela_Cruz" title="Juan dela Cruz">Juan dela Cruz</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">In this <a href="/wiki/Spanish_name" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish name">Spanish name</a>, the first or paternal&#32;<a href="/wiki/Surname" title="Surname">surname</a> is <i> de Yepes</i>&#32;and the second or maternal family name is <i> Álvarez</i>.</div>

class="hlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux" class="mw-redirect" title="Thérèse de Lisieux">Thérèse de Lisieux</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Edith_Stein" title="Edith Stein">Edith <ul><li><i><a href="/wiki/Spiritual_Canticle" title="Spiritual Canticle">Spiritual Canticle</a></i></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Dark_Night_of_the_Soul" title="Dark Night of the Soul">Dark Night of the Soul</a></i></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Ascent_of_Mount_Carmel" title="Ascent of Mount Carmel">Ascent of Mount Carmel</a></i></li></ul>
</div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>John of the Cross</b>, <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">OCD</a> (Spanish: <i lang="es">Juan de la Cruz</i>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ioannes a Cruce</i>; born <b>Juan de Yepes y Álvarez</b>; 24 June 1542 – 14 December 1591) was a Spanish <a href="/wiki/Catholic_priest" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic priest">Catholic priest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystic</a>, and a <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">Carmelite</a> <a href="/wiki/Friar" title="Friar">friar</a> of <a href="/wiki/Converso" title="Converso">converso</a> origin. He is a major figure of the <a href="/wiki/Counter-Reformation" title="Counter-Reformation">Counter-Reformation</a> in Spain, and he is one of the thirty-seven <a href="/wiki/Doctors_of_the_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctors of the Church">Doctors of the Church</a>.
</p><p>John of the Cross is known for his writings. He was mentored by and corresponded with the older Carmelite, <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a>. Both his poetry and his studies on the development of the <a href="/wiki/Soul" title="Soul">soul</a> are considered the summit of <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystical</a> Spanish literature and among the greatest works of all <a href="/wiki/Spanish_literature" title="Spanish literature">Spanish literature</a>. He was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XIII" title="Pope Benedict XIII">Pope Benedict XIII</a> in 1726. In 1926, he was declared a Doctor of the Church by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a>, and is also known as the "mystical doctor".
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:#ddf;text-align:center;">Literature&#160;<b>·</b> Media</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content" style="padding-left:0.5em;padding-right:0.5em;;font-style:italic;">

<li><a href="/wiki/Fatima_(2020_film)" title="Fatima (2020 film)">Fatima</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>He was born <b>Juan de Yepes y Álvarez</b> at <a href="/wiki/Fontiveros" title="Fontiveros">Fontiveros</a>, <a href="/wiki/Old_Castile" title="Old Castile">Old Castile</a> into a <a href="/wiki/Converso" title="Converso">converso</a> family (descendants of Jewish converts to Catholicism) in Fontiveros, near <a href="/wiki/%C3%81vila,_Spain" class="mw-redirect" title="Ávila, Spain">Ávila</a>, a town of around 2,000 people.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson200227_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson200227-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> His father, Gonzalo, was an accountant to richer relatives who were silk merchants. In 1529 Gonzalo married John's mother, Catalina, who was an orphan of a lower class; he was rejected by his family and forced to work with his wife as a weaver.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETillyer19844_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETillyer19844-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> John's father died in 1545, while John was still only around three years old.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Two years later, John's older brother, Luis, died, probably as a result of malnourishment due to the poverty to which the family had been reduced. As a result, John's mother Catalina took John and his surviving brother Francisco, first to Arévalo, in 1548 and then in 1551 to <a href="/wiki/Medina_del_Campo" title="Medina del Campo">Medina del Campo</a>, where she was able to find work.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-impact_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-impact-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Medina, John entered a school for 160<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson200231_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson200231-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> poor children, mostly orphans, to receive a basic education, mainly in Christian doctrine. They were given some food, clothing and lodging. While studying there, he was chosen to serve as an altar boy at a nearby monastery of <a href="/wiki/Augustinians" title="Augustinians">Augustinian</a> nuns.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Growing up, John worked at a hospital and studied the humanities at a <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">Jesuit</a> school from 1559 to 1563. The Society of Jesus was at that time a new organisation, having been founded only a few years earlier by the Spaniard St. <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Loyola" title="Ignatius of Loyola">Ignatius of Loyola</a>. In 1563 he entered the Carmelite Order, adopting the name <b>John of St. Matthias</b>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The following year, in 1564 he made his First Profession as a Carmelite and travelled to <a href="/wiki/Salamanca" title="Salamanca">Salamanca</a> University, where he studied theology and philosophy.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> There he met <a href="/wiki/Luis_Ponce_de_Le%C3%B3n" class="mw-redirect" title="Luis Ponce de León">Fray Luis de León</a>, who taught biblical studies (<a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">Exegesis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aramaic" title="Aramaic">Aramaic</a>) at the university.
</p>
<h3><span id="Joining_the_Reform_of_Teresa_of_.C3.81vila"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Joining_the_Reform_of_Teresa_of_Ávila">Joining the Reform of Teresa of Ávila</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Joining the Reform of Teresa of Ávila">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:S._Juan_de_la_Cruz_y_Santa_Teresa.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/S._Juan_de_la_Cruz_y_Santa_Teresa.JPG/220px-S._Juan_de_la_Cruz_y_Santa_Teresa.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/S._Juan_de_la_Cruz_y_Santa_Teresa.JPG/330px-S._Juan_de_la_Cruz_y_Santa_Teresa.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/S._Juan_de_la_Cruz_y_Santa_Teresa.JPG/440px-S._Juan_de_la_Cruz_y_Santa_Teresa.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2148" data-file-height="1707" /></a><figcaption>Statues representing John of the Cross and <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a> in Beas de Segura</figcaption></figure>
<p>John was <a href="/wiki/Holy_Orders" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Orders">ordained</a> as a priest in 1567. He subsequently thought about joining the strict <a href="/wiki/Carthusian" class="mw-redirect" title="Carthusian">Carthusian</a> Order, which appealed to him because of its practice of solitary and silent contemplation. His journey from Salamanca to <a href="/wiki/Medina_del_Campo" title="Medina del Campo">Medina del Campo</a>, probably in September 1567 became pivotal.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> In Medina he met the influential Carmelite nun, <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a>. She was staying in Medina to found the second of her new convents.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> She immediately talked to him about her reformation projects for the Order: she was seeking to restore the purity of the Carmelite Order by reverting to the observance of its "Primitive Rule" of 1209, which had been relaxed by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Eugene_IV" title="Pope Eugene IV">Pope Eugene IV</a> in 1432.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Under the Rule, much of the day and night were to be divided between the recitation of the <a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_the_Hours" title="Liturgy of the Hours">Liturgy of the Hours</a>, study and devotional reading, the celebration of Mass and periods of solitude. In the case of friars, time was to be spent evangelizing the population around the monastery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETillyer19848_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETillyer19848-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> There was to be total <a href="/wiki/Abstinence" title="Abstinence">abstinence</a> from meat and a lengthy period of <a href="/wiki/Fasting" title="Fasting">fasting</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Feast_of_the_Cross#September_14" title="Feast of the Cross">Feast of the Exaltation of the Cross</a> (14 September) until Easter. There were to be long periods of silence, especially between <a href="/wiki/Compline" title="Compline">Compline</a> and <a href="/wiki/Prime_(liturgy)" title="Prime (liturgy)">Prime</a>. More simple, that is coarser, shorter habits were to be adopted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHardy198227_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardy198227-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> There was also an <a href="/wiki/Injunction" title="Injunction">injunction</a> against wearing covered shoes (also previously mitigated in 1432). That particular observance distinguished the "<a href="/wiki/Discalced" title="Discalced">discalced</a>", i.e., barefoot followers of Teresa from traditional Carmelites, and they would be formally recognized as the separate <i>Order of <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">Discalced Carmelites</a></i> in 1580.
</p><p>Teresa asked John to delay his entry into the Carthusian order and to follow her. Having spent a final year studying in Salamanca, in August 1568 John travelled with Teresa from Medina to <a href="/wiki/Valladolid" title="Valladolid">Valladolid</a>, where Teresa intended to found another convent. After a spell at Teresa's side in Valladolid, learning more about the new form of Carmelite life, in October 1568, John left Valladolid, accompanied by Friar Antonio de Jesús de Heredia, to found a new monastery for Carmelite friars, the first to follow Teresa's principles. They were given the use of a derelict house at <a href="/wiki/Duruelo" title="Duruelo">Duruelo</a>, which had been donated to Teresa. On 28 November 1568, the monastery was established, and on that same day, John changed his name to "John of the Cross".<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Soon after, in June 1570, the friars found the house at Duruelo was too small, and so moved to the nearby town of <a href="/wiki/Mancera_de_Abajo" title="Mancera de Abajo">Mancera de Abajo</a>, midway between Ávila and Salamanca. John moved from the first community to set up a new community at <a href="/wiki/Pastrana,_Spain" title="Pastrana, Spain">Pastrana</a> in October 1570, and then a further community at <a href="/wiki/Alcal%C3%A1_de_Henares" title="Alcalá de Henares">Alcalá de Henares</a>, as a house for the academic training of the friars. In 1572 he arrived in Ávila, at Teresa's invitation. She had been appointed <a href="/wiki/Prior_(ecclesiastical)" title="Prior (ecclesiastical)">prioress</a> of the Convent of the Incarnation there in 1571.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> John became the <a href="/wiki/Spiritual_director" class="mw-redirect" title="Spiritual director">spiritual director</a> and <a href="/wiki/Confessor" title="Confessor">confessor</a> of Teresa and the other 130 nuns there, as well as for a wide range of laypeople in the city.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> In 1574, John accompanied Teresa for the foundation of a new religious community in <a href="/wiki/Segovia" title="Segovia">Segovia</a>, returning to Ávila after staying there a week. Aside from the one trip, John seems to have remained in Ávila between 1572 and 1577.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHardy198256_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardy198256-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_of_the_Cross_crucifixion_sketch.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/John_of_the_Cross_crucifixion_sketch.jpg/170px-John_of_the_Cross_crucifixion_sketch.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="203" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/John_of_the_Cross_crucifixion_sketch.jpg/255px-John_of_the_Cross_crucifixion_sketch.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/John_of_the_Cross_crucifixion_sketch.jpg/340px-John_of_the_Cross_crucifixion_sketch.jpg 2x" data-file-width="377" data-file-height="450" /></a><figcaption>Drawing of the crucifixion by John of the Cross</figcaption></figure>
<p>At some time between 1574 and 1577, while praying in a loft overlooking the sanctuary in the Monastery of the Incarnation in Ávila, John had a vision of the <a href="/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus" title="Crucifixion of Jesus">crucified Christ</a>, which led him to create his drawing of Christ "from above". In 1641, this drawing was placed in a small <a href="/wiki/Monstrance" title="Monstrance">monstrance</a> and kept in Ávila. This same drawing inspired the artist <a href="/wiki/Salvador_Dal%C3%AD" title="Salvador Dalí">Salvador Dalí</a>'s 1951 work <i><a href="/wiki/Christ_of_Saint_John_of_the_Cross" title="Christ of Saint John of the Cross">Christ of Saint John of the Cross</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Height_of_Carmelite_tensions">Height of Carmelite tensions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Height of Carmelite tensions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The years 1575–77 saw a great increase in tensions among Spanish Carmelite friars over the reforms of Teresa and John. Since 1566 the reforms had been overseen by <a href="/wiki/Canonical_visitation" title="Canonical visitation">Canonical Visitors</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican Order</a>, with one appointed to Castile and a second to <a href="/wiki/Andalusia" title="Andalusia">Andalusia</a>. The Visitors had substantial powers: they could move members of religious communities from one house to another or from one <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastical_province" title="Ecclesiastical province">province</a> to the next. They could assist <a href="/wiki/Religious_superior" class="mw-redirect" title="Religious superior">religious superiors</a> in the discharge of their office, and could delegate superiors between the Dominican or Carmelite orders. In Castile, the Visitor was Pedro Fernández, who prudently balanced the interests of the Discalced Carmelites with those of the nuns and friars who did not desire reform.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Andalusia to the south, the Visitor was Francisco Vargas, and tensions rose due to his clear preference for the Discalced friars. Vargas asked them to make foundations in various cities, in contradiction to the express orders from the Carmelite <a href="/wiki/Superior_general" class="mw-redirect" title="Superior general">Prior General</a> to curb expansion in Andalusia. As a result, a <a href="/wiki/General_Chapter" class="mw-redirect" title="General Chapter">General Chapter</a> of the Carmelite Order was convened at <a href="/wiki/Piacenza" title="Piacenza">Piacenza</a> in Italy in May 1576, out of concern that events in Spain were getting out of hand. It concluded by ordering the total suppression of the Discalced houses.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>That measure was not immediately enforced. <a href="/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain" title="Philip II of Spain">King Philip II of Spain</a> was supportive of Teresa's reforms, and so was not immediately willing to grant the necessary permission to enforce the ordinance. The Discalced friars also found support from the papal <a href="/wiki/Nuncio" title="Nuncio">nuncio</a> to Spain, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicol%C3%B2_Ormaneto&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Nicolò Ormaneto (page does not exist)">Nicolò Ormaneto</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%B2_Ormaneto" class="extiw" title="it:Nicolò Ormaneto">it</a>&#93;</span>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Padua" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Padua">Bishop of Padua</a>, who still had ultimate power to visit and reform religious orders. When asked by the Discalced friars to intervene, Nuncio Ormaneto replaced Vargas as Visitor of the Carmelites in Andalusia with <a href="/wiki/Jer%C3%B3nimo_Graci%C3%A1n" class="mw-redirect" title="Jerónimo Gracián">Jerónimo Gracián</a>, a priest from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Alcal%C3%A1" title="University of Alcalá">University of Alcalá</a>, who was in fact a Discalced Carmelite friar himself.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The nuncio's protection helped John avoid problems for a time. In January 1576, John was detained in Medina del Campo by traditional Carmelite friars, but through the nuncio's intervention, he was soon released.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> When Ormaneto died on 18 June 1577, John was left without protection, and the friars opposing his reforms regained the upper hand.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Foundations.2C_imprisonment.2C_torture_and_death"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foundations,_imprisonment,_torture_and_death">Foundations, imprisonment, torture and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Foundations, imprisonment, torture and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:El_Greco_-_View_of_Toledo_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/El_Greco_-_View_of_Toledo_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/170px-El_Greco_-_View_of_Toledo_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/El_Greco_-_View_of_Toledo_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/255px-El_Greco_-_View_of_Toledo_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/El_Greco_-_View_of_Toledo_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/340px-El_Greco_-_View_of_Toledo_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3571" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/El_Greco" title="El Greco">El Greco</a>'s landscape of Toledo depicts the priory in which John was held captive, just below the old <i><a href="/wiki/Alc%C3%A1zar_of_Toledo" title="Alcázar of Toledo">alcázar</a></i> (fort) and perched on the banks of the Tajo on high cliffs.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On the night of 2 December 1577, a group of Carmelites opposed to reform broke into John's dwelling in Ávila and took him prisoner. John had received an order from superiors, opposed to reform, to leave Ávila and return to his original house. John had refused on the basis that his reform work had been approved by the papal nuncio to Spain, a higher authority than these superiors.<sup id="cite_ref-ascent_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ascent-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The Carmelites therefore took John captive. John was taken from Ávila to the Carmelite monastery in <a href="/wiki/Toledo,_Spain" title="Toledo, Spain">Toledo</a>, at that time the order's leading monastery in Castile, with a community of 40 friars.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson200248_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson200248-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John was brought before a court of friars, accused of disobeying the ordinances of Piacenza. Despite his argument that he had not disobeyed the ordinances, he was sentenced to a term of imprisonment. He was jailed in a monastery where he was kept under a brutal regime that included public lashings before the community at least weekly, and severe isolation in a tiny stifling cell measuring barely 10 feet by 6 feet. Except when rarely permitted an oil lamp, he had to stand on a bench to read his <a href="/wiki/Breviary" title="Breviary">breviary</a> by the light through the hole into the adjoining room. He had no change of clothing and a penitential diet of water, bread and scraps of salt fish.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETillyer198410_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETillyer198410-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> During his imprisonment, he composed a great part of his most famous poem <i><a href="/wiki/Spiritual_Canticle" title="Spiritual Canticle">Spiritual Canticle</a></i>, as well as a few shorter poems. The paper was passed to him by the friar who guarded his cell.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> He managed to escape eight months later, on 15 August 1578, through a small window in a room adjoining his cell. (He had managed to pry open the hinges of the cell door earlier that day.)<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>After being nursed back to health, first by Teresa's nuns in <a href="/wiki/Toledo,_Spain" title="Toledo, Spain">Toledo</a>, and then during six weeks at the Hospital of Santa Cruz, John continued with the reforms. In October 1578 he joined a meeting at <a href="/wiki/Almod%C3%B3var_del_Campo" title="Almodóvar del Campo">Almodóvar del Campo</a> of reform supporters, better known as the Discalced Carmelites.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> There, in part as a result of the opposition faced from other Carmelites, they decided to request from the Pope their formal separation from the rest of the Carmelite order.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At that meeting John was appointed superior of El Calvario, an isolated monastery of around thirty friars in the mountains about 6 miles away<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson2002117_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson2002117-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> from <a href="/wiki/Beas,_Spain" title="Beas, Spain">Beas</a> in Andalusia. During that time he befriended the nun, <a href="/wiki/Ana_de_Jes%C3%BAs" title="Ana de Jesús">Ana de Jesús</a>, superior of the Discalced nuns at Beas, through his visits to the town every Saturday. While at El Calvario he composed the first version of his commentary on his poem, <i>The Spiritual Canticle</i>, possibly at the request of the nuns in Beas.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 1579 he moved to <a href="/wiki/Baeza,_Spain" title="Baeza, Spain">Baeza</a>, a town of around 50,000 people, to serve as <a href="/wiki/Rector_(academic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Rector (academic)">rector</a> of a new college, the Colegio de San Basilio, for Discalced friars in Andalusia. It opened on 13 June 1579. He remained in post until 1582, spending much of his time as a spiritual director to the friars and townspeople.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>1580 was a significant year in the resolution of disputes between the Carmelites. On 22 June, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XIII" title="Pope Gregory XIII">Pope Gregory XIII</a> signed a decree, entitled <i>Pia Consideratione</i>, which authorised the separation of the old (later "calced") and the newly reformed, "Discalced" Carmelites. The Dominican friar <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_%C3%81vila#Leadership" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Ávila">Juan Velázquez de las Cuevas</a> was appointed to oversee the decision. At the first General Chapter of the Discalced Carmelites, in <a href="/wiki/Alcal%C3%A1_de_Henares" title="Alcalá de Henares">Alcalá de Henares</a> on 3 March 1581, John of the Cross was elected one of the "Definitors" of the community, and wrote a constitution for them. By the time of the Provincial Chapter at Alcalá in 1581, there were 22 houses, some 300 friars and 200 nuns among the Discalced Carmelites.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson2002119_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson2002119-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sepulcro-de-san-juan-de-la-cruz-02.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Sepulcro-de-san-juan-de-la-cruz-02.jpg/170px-Sepulcro-de-san-juan-de-la-cruz-02.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="254" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Sepulcro-de-san-juan-de-la-cruz-02.jpg/255px-Sepulcro-de-san-juan-de-la-cruz-02.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Sepulcro-de-san-juan-de-la-cruz-02.jpg/340px-Sepulcro-de-san-juan-de-la-cruz-02.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1416" data-file-height="2116" /></a><figcaption>Saint John of the Cross' shrine and reliquary, Convent of Carmelite Friars, Segovia</figcaption></figure> <figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JohnCrossRelicsUbeda.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/JohnCrossRelicsUbeda.jpg/220px-JohnCrossRelicsUbeda.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/JohnCrossRelicsUbeda.jpg/330px-JohnCrossRelicsUbeda.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/JohnCrossRelicsUbeda.jpg/440px-JohnCrossRelicsUbeda.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="683" /></a><figcaption>Reliquary of John of the Cross in Úbeda, Spain</figcaption></figure>
<p>In November 1581, John was sent by Teresa to help Ana de Jesús to found a convent in <a href="/wiki/Granada" title="Granada">Granada</a>. Arriving in January 1582, she set up a convent, while John stayed in the monastery of Los Mártires, near the Alhambra, becoming its prior in March 1582.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHardy198290_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardy198290-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> While there, he learned of Teresa's death in October of that year.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In February 1585, John travelled to <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A1laga" title="Málaga">Málaga</a> where he established a convent for Discalced nuns. In May 1585, at the General Chapter of the Discalced Carmelites in <a href="/wiki/Lisbon" title="Lisbon">Lisbon</a>, John was elected Vicar Provincial of Andalusia, a post which required him to travel frequently, making annual visitations to the houses of friars and nuns in Andalusia. During this time he founded seven new monasteries in the region, and is estimated to have travelled around 25,000&#160;km.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson2002122_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson2002122-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 1588, he was elected third Councillor to the Vicar General for the Discalced Carmelites, Father Nicolas Doria. To fulfill this role, he had to return to Segovia in Castile, where he also took on the role of prior of the monastery. After disagreeing in 1590–1 with some of Doria's remodelling of the leadership of the Discalced Carmelite Order, John was removed from his post in Segovia, and sent by Doria in June 1591 to an isolated monastery in Andalusia called La Peñuela. There he fell ill, and travelled to the monastery at <a href="/wiki/%C3%9Abeda,_Spain" class="mw-redirect" title="Úbeda, Spain">Úbeda</a> for treatment. His condition worsened, however, and he died there, of <a href="/wiki/Erysipelas" title="Erysipelas">erysipelas</a> on 14 December 1591.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The morning after John's death huge numbers of townspeople in Úbeda entered the monastery to view his body; in the crush, many were able to take home bits of his habit. He was initially buried at Úbeda, but, at the request of the monastery in Segovia, his body was secretly moved there in 1593. The people of Úbeda, however, unhappy at this change, sent a representative to petition the pope to move the body back to its original resting place. Pope Clement VIII, impressed by the petition, issued a Brief on 15 October 1596 ordering the return of the body to Úbeda. Eventually, in a compromise, the superiors of the Discalced Carmelites decided that the monastery at Úbeda would receive one leg and one arm of the corpse from Segovia (the monastery at Úbeda had already kept one leg in 1593, and the other arm had been removed as the corpse passed through Madrid in 1593, to form a relic there). A hand and a leg remain visible in a reliquary at the Oratory of San Juan de la Cruz in Úbeda, a monastery built in 1627 though connected to the original Discalced monastery in the town founded in 1587.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHardy1982113–130_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardy1982113–130-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The head and torso were retained by the monastery at Segovia. They were venerated until 1647, when on orders from Rome designed to prevent the veneration of remains without official approval, the remains were buried in the ground. In the 1930s they were disinterred, and are now sited in a side chapel in a marble case above a special altar.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHardy1982113–130_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardy1982113–130-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Proceedings to beatify John began between 1614 and 1616. He was eventually <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> in 1675 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_X" title="Pope Clement X">Pope Clement X</a>, and was <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonized</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XIII" title="Pope Benedict XIII">Benedict XIII</a> in 1726. When his feast day was added to the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> in 1738, it was assigned to 24 November, since his date of death was impeded by the then-existing <a href="/wiki/Octave_(liturgical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Octave (liturgical)">octave</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Feast_of_the_Immaculate_Conception" title="Feast of the Immaculate Conception">Feast of the Immaculate Conception</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> This obstacle was removed in 1955 and in 1969 <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> moved it to the <i>dies natalis</i> (birthday to heaven) of John, <a href="/wiki/December_14" title="December 14">14 December</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> honor him on <a href="/wiki/December_14" title="December 14">the same date</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-lff2018_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lff2018-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In 1926, he was declared a Doctor of the Church by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a> after the definitive consultation of <a href="/wiki/Reginald_Garrigou-Lagrange" class="mw-redirect" title="Reginald Garrigou-Lagrange">Reginald Garrigou-Lagrange</a> <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">O.P.</a>, professor of philosophy and theology at the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_University_of_Saint_Thomas_Aquinas" title="Pontifical University of Saint Thomas Aquinas">Pontifical University of Saint Thomas Aquinas</a>, <i>Angelicum</i> in Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Literary_works">Literary works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Literary works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:El_Monte_Carmelo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/El_Monte_Carmelo.jpg/170px-El_Monte_Carmelo.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="249" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/El_Monte_Carmelo.jpg/255px-El_Monte_Carmelo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/El_Monte_Carmelo.jpg/340px-El_Monte_Carmelo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="873" data-file-height="1277" /></a><figcaption>The Ascent of Mount Carmel, as depicted in the first edition of 1618 by Diego de Astor<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure><p> John of the Cross is considered one of the foremost poets in Spanish. Although his complete poems add up to fewer than 2500 verses, two of them, the <i><a href="/wiki/Spiritual_Canticle" title="Spiritual Canticle">Spiritual Canticle</a></i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Dark_Night_of_the_Soul" title="Dark Night of the Soul">Dark Night of the Soul</a></i>, are widely considered masterpieces of Spanish poetry, both for their formal <a href="/wiki/Stylistics_(linguistics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Stylistics (linguistics)">style</a> and their rich <a href="/wiki/Symbol" title="Symbol">symbolism</a> and imagery. His theological works often consist of commentaries on the poems. All the works were written between 1578 and his death in 1591.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The <i>Spiritual Canticle</i> is an <a href="/wiki/Eclogue" title="Eclogue">eclogue</a> in which the bride, representing the <a href="/wiki/Soul_(spirit)" class="mw-redirect" title="Soul (spirit)">soul</a>, searches for the bridegroom, representing <a href="/wiki/Christian_views_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian views of Jesus">Jesus Christ</a>, and is anxious at having lost him. Both are filled with joy upon reuniting. It can be seen as a free-form Spanish version of the <a href="/wiki/Song_of_Songs" title="Song of Songs">Song of Songs</a> at a time when vernacular translations of the <a href="/wiki/Bible" title="Bible">Bible</a> were forbidden. The first 31 stanzas of the poem were composed in 1578 while John was imprisoned in Toledo. After his escape it was read by the nuns at Beas, who made copies of the stanzas. Over the following years, John added further lines. Today, two versions exist: one with 39 stanzas and one with 40 with some of the stanzas ordered differently. The first redaction of the commentary on the poem was written in 1584, at the request of Madre Ana de Jesús, when she was prioress of the Discalced Carmelite nuns in Granada. A second edition, which contains more detail, was written in 1585–6.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i>Dark Night</i>, from which the phrase <i><a href="/wiki/Dark_Night_of_the_Soul" title="Dark Night of the Soul">Dark Night of the Soul</a></i> takes its name, narrates the journey of the soul from its bodily home to union with God. It happens during the "dark", which represents the hardships and difficulties met in detachment from the world and reaching the light of the union with the Creator. There are several steps during the state of darkness, which are described in successive stanzas. The main idea behind the poem is the painful experience required to attain spiritual maturity and union with God. The poem was likely written in 1578 or 1579. In 1584–5, John wrote a commentary on the first two stanzas and on the first line of the third stanza.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Ascent_of_Mount_Carmel" title="Ascent of Mount Carmel">Ascent of Mount Carmel</a></i> is a more systematic study of the ascetical endeavour of a soul seeking perfect union with God and the <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystical</a> events encountered along the way. Although it begins as a commentary on <i>The Dark Night</i>, after the first two stanzas of the poem, it rapidly diverts into a full treatise. It was composed some time between 1581 and 1585.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaug_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaug-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A four-stanza work, <i>Living Flame of Love</i>, describes a greater <a href="/wiki/Intimacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Intimacy">intimacy</a>, as the soul responds to God's love. It was written in a first version at Granada between 1585 and 1586, apparently in two weeks, and in a mostly identical second version at La Peñuela in 1591.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanau_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanau-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>These, together with his <i>Dichos de Luz y Amor</i> or "Sayings of Light and Love" along with Teresa's own writings, are the most important mystical works in Spanish, and have deeply influenced later spiritual writers across the world. They include: <a href="/wiki/T._S._Eliot" title="T. S. Eliot">T. S. Eliot</a>, <a href="/wiki/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux" class="mw-redirect" title="Thérèse de Lisieux">Thérèse de Lisieux</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edith_Stein" title="Edith Stein">Edith Stein</a> (Teresa Benedicta of the Cross) and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Merton" title="Thomas Merton">Thomas Merton</a>. John is said to have also influenced philosophers (<a href="/wiki/Jacques_Maritain" title="Jacques Maritain">Jacques Maritain</a>), theologians (<a href="/wiki/Hans_Urs_von_Balthasar" title="Hans Urs von Balthasar">Hans Urs von Balthasar</a>), <a href="/wiki/Pacifists" class="mw-redirect" title="Pacifists">pacifists</a> (<a href="/wiki/Dorothy_Day" title="Dorothy Day">Dorothy Day</a>, <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Berrigan" title="Daniel Berrigan">Daniel Berrigan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Philip_Berrigan" title="Philip Berrigan">Philip Berrigan</a>) and artists (<a href="/wiki/Salvador_Dal%C3%AD" title="Salvador Dalí">Salvador Dalí</a>). <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> wrote his theological dissertation on the mystical theology of John of the Cross.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Editions_of_his_works">Editions of his works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Editions of his works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>His writings were first published in 1618 by Diego de Salablanca. The numerical divisions in the work, still used by modern editions of the text, were introduced by Salablanca (they were not in John's original writings) to help make the work more manageable for the reader.<sup id="cite_ref-Kavanaugh_10-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kavanaugh-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> This edition does not contain the <i>Spiritual Canticle</i> however, and also omits or adapts certain passages, perhaps for fear of falling foul of the Inquisition.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i>Spiritual Canticle</i> was first included in the 1630 edition, produced by Fray Jeronimo de San José, at Madrid. This edition was largely followed by later editors, although editions in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries gradually included a few more poems and letters.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first French edition was published in Paris in 1622,<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> and the first Castilian edition in 1627 in Brussels.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A critical edition of St John of the Cross's work in English was published by <a href="/wiki/Edgar_Allison_Peers" title="Edgar Allison Peers">E. Allison Peers</a> in 1935.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Intellectual_influences">Intellectual influences</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Intellectual influences">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The influences on John's writing are subject to an ongoing debate.
It is widely acknowledged that at Salamanca university there would have existed a range of intellectual positions. In John's time they included the influences of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>, of <a href="/wiki/Duns_Scotus" title="Duns Scotus">Scotus</a> and of <a href="/wiki/Guillaume_Durand" title="Guillaume Durand">Durandus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> It is often assumed that John would have absorbed the thought of Aquinas, to explain the scholastic framework of his writings.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>However, the belief that John was taught at both the Carmelite College of San Andrès and at the University of Salamanca has been challenged.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Bezares calls into question whether John even studied theology at the University of Salamanca. The philosophy courses John probably took in logic, natural and moral philosophy, can be reconstructed, but Bezares argues that John in fact abandoned his studies at Salamanca in 1568 to join Teresa, rather than graduating.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In the first biography of John, published in 1628, it is claimed, on the basis of information from John's fellow students, that he in 1567 made a special study of mystical writers, in particular of Pseudo-Dionysius and <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_I" title="Pope Gregory I">Pope Gregory I</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
There is little consensus from John's early years or potential influences.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Scripture">Scripture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Scripture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John was influenced heavily by the Bible. Scriptural images are common in both his poems and prose. In total, there are 1,583 explicit and 115 implicit quotations from the Bible in his works.<sup id="cite_ref-Tyler_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tyler-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> The influence of the <i>Song of Songs</i> on John's <i>Spiritual Canticle</i> has often been noted, both in terms of the structure of the poem, with its dialogue between two lovers, the account of their difficulties in meeting each other and the "offstage chorus" that comments on the action, and also in terms of the imagery for example, of pomegranates, wine cellar, turtle dove and lilies, which echoes that of the <i>Song of Songs</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Tyler_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tyler-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition, John shows at occasional points the influence of the <a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_the_Hours" title="Liturgy of the Hours">Divine Office</a>. This demonstrates how John, steeped in the language and rituals of the Church, drew at times on the phrases and language here.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pseudo-Dionysius">Pseudo-Dionysius</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Pseudo-Dionysius">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>It has rarely been disputed that the overall structure of John's mystical theology, and his language of the union of the soul with God, is influenced by the pseudo-Dionysian tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> However, it has not been clear whether John might have had direct access to the writings of <a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Dionysius" class="mw-redirect" title="Pseudo-Dionysius">Pseudo-Dionysius</a>, or whether this influence may have been mediated through various later authors.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Medieval_mystics">Medieval mystics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Medieval mystics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>It is widely acknowledged that John may have been influenced by the writings of other medieval mystics, though there is debate about the exact thought which may have influenced him, and about how he might have been exposed to their ideas.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The possibility of influence by the so-called "<a href="/wiki/Rhineland_mystics" class="mw-redirect" title="Rhineland mystics">Rhineland mystics</a>" such as <a href="/wiki/Meister_Eckhart" title="Meister Eckhart">Meister Eckhart</a>, <a href="/wiki/Johannes_Tauler" title="Johannes Tauler">Johannes Tauler</a>, <a href="/wiki/Henry_Suso" title="Henry Suso">Henry Suso</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_of_Ruysbroeck" class="mw-redirect" title="John of Ruysbroeck">John of Ruysbroeck</a> has also been mooted by many authors.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Secular_Spanish_poetry">Secular Spanish poetry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Secular Spanish poetry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>A strong argument can also be made for contemporary Spanish literary influences on John. This case was first made in detail by Dámaso Alonso, who believed that as well as drawing from scripture, John was transforming non-religious, profane themes, derived from popular songs (<i>romanceros</i>) into religious poetry.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Islamic_influence">Islamic influence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Islamic influence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>A controversial theory on the origins of John's mystical imagery is that he may have been influenced by Islamic sources. This was first proposed in detail by Miguel Asín Palacios and has been most recently put forward by the <a href="/wiki/Puerto_Rico" title="Puerto Rico">Puerto Rican</a> scholar <a href="/wiki/Luce_L%C3%B3pez-Baralt" title="Luce López-Baralt">Luce López-Baralt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Arguing that John was influenced by Islamic sources on the peninsula, she traces Islamic antecedents of the images of the "dark night", the "solitary bird" of the <i>Spiritual Canticle</i>, wine and mystical intoxication (the <i>Spiritual Canticle</i>), lamps of fire (the <i>Living Flame</i>). However, Peter Tyler concludes, there "are sufficient Christian medieval antecedents for many of the metaphors John employs to suggest we should look for Christian sources rather than Muslim sources".<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> As José Nieto indicates, in trying to locate a link between Spanish Christian mysticism and Islamic mysticism, it might make more sense to refer to the common Neo-Platonic tradition and mystical experiences of both, rather than seek direct influence.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Books">Books</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_the_Cross&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Books">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

Hardy, <i>The Life of St John of the Cross: Search for Nothing</i> (London: DLT, 1982), p. 24, argues that the reason was for John to say his first mass</span>

<li id="cite_note-22"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-22">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://aleteia.org/2017/09/22/discover-the-crucifix-drawn-by-saint-john-of-the-cross-after-a-mystical-vision/">"Discover the crucifix drawn by St. John of the Cross after a mystical vision"</a>. <i>Aleteia — Catholic Spirituality, Lifestyle, World News, and Culture</i>. 22 September 2017<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">11 May</span> 2021</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=Aleteia+%E2%80%94+Catholic+Spirituality%2C+Lifestyle%2C+World+News%2C+and+Culture&amp;rft.atitle=Discover+the+crucifix+drawn+by+St.+John+of+the+Cross+after+a+mystical+vision&amp;rft.date=2017-09-22&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Faleteia.org%2F2017%2F09%2F22%2Fdiscover-the-crucifix-drawn-by-saint-john-of-the-cross-after-a-mystical-vision%2F&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJohn+of+the+Cross" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-23"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-23">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">He is possibly the same Pedro Fernández who became the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_%C3%81vila#Leadership" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Ávila">Bishop of Ávila</a> in 1581. He who appointed Teresa as prioress in Ávila in 1571, while also maintaining good relations with the Carmelite <a href="/wiki/Provincial_superior" title="Provincial superior">Prior Provincial</a> of Castile.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-24"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-24">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Kavanaugh (1991) states that this was all the Discalced houses founded in Andalusia. E. Allison Peers, <i>Complete Works</i>, Vol. I, p. xxvii (1943) states that this was all the Discalced monasteries but two.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-ascent-25"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-ascent_25-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFBennedict_Zimmermann" class="citation web cs1">Bennedict Zimmermann. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/ascentofmountcar00johnuoft">"Ascent of Mt. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">11 December</span> 2009</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=Ascent+of+Mt.+Carmel%2C+introductory+essay+THE+DEVELOPMENT+OF+MYSTICISM+IN+THE+CARMELITE+ORDER&amp;rft.pub=Thomas+Baker+and+Internet+Archive&amp;rft.au=Bennedict+Zimmermann&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Farchive.org%2Fdetails%2Fascentofmountcar00johnuoft&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJohn+of+the+Cross" class="Z3988"></span>

<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson200248-26"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson200248_26-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFThompson2002">Thompson 2002</a>, p.&#160;48. Thompson points out that many earlier biographers have stated the number of friars at Toledo to be 80, but this is simply taken from Crisogono's Spanish biography. Alain Cugno (1982) gives the number of friars as 800 — which Thompson assumes this must be a misprint. However, as Thompson details, the actual number of friars has been reconstructed from comparing various extant documents that in 1576, 42 friars belonged to the house, with only about 23 of them resident, the remainder being absent for various reasons. This is done by J. Carlos Vuzeute Mendoza, 'La prisión de San Juan de la Cruz: El convent del Carmen de Toledo en 1577 y 1578', A. García Simón, ed, <i>Actas del congreso internacional sanjuanista</i>, 3 vols. (Valladolid: Junta de Castilla y León, 1993) II, pp. 427–436</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-27"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-27">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Peter Tyler, <i>St John of the Cross</i> (New York: Continuum, 2000), p. 28. The reference to the El Greco painting is also taken from here. The priory no longer exists, having been destroyed in 1936 — it is now the Toledo Municipal car park.</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-40"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-40">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Eric Truman Dicken, <i>The Crucible of Love</i>, (1963), pp. 238–242, points out that this image is neither a true representation of John's thought, nor is it true to the image drawn by John himself of the 'Mount'. This latter image was first published in 1929, from a 1759 copy of the original (now lost) almost certainly drawn by John himself. It is the 1618 image, though, which was influential on later depictions of the 'Mount', such as in the 1748 Venice edition and 1858 Genoa editions of John's work.</span>

</li>
<li id="cite_note-56"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-56">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">However, there is little precise agreement on which particular mystics may have been influential. <a href="/wiki/Jean_Orcibal" title="Jean Orcibal">Jean Orcibal</a>, <i>S Jean de la Croix et les mystiques Rheno-Flamands</i> (Desclee-Brouwer, Presence du Carmel, no. 6); Crisogono (1929), I, 17, believed that John was influenced more by German mysticism, than perhaps by <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nyssa" title="Gregory of Nyssa">Gregory of Nyssa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Dionysius_the_Areopagite" title="Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite">Pseudo-Dionysius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine of Hippo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bernard_of_Clairvaux" title="Bernard of Clairvaux">Bernard of Clairvaux</a>, the <a href="/wiki/School_of_Saint_Victor" title="School of Saint Victor">School of Saint Victor</a> and the <i><a href="/wiki/The_Imitation_of_Christ" title="The Imitation of Christ">Imitation</a></i>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-57"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-57">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Dámaso Alonso, <i>La poesía de San Juan de la Cruz</i> (Madrid, 1942)</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-58"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-58">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Luce Lopez Baralt, <i>Juan de la Cruz y el Islam</i> (1990)</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-59"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-59">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Peter Tyler, <i>St John of the Cross</i> (2010), pp. 138–142</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-60"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-60">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">José Nieto, <i>Mystic, Rebel, Saint: A Study of St. John of the Cross</i> (Geneva, 1979)</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
